Declarative distributed broadcast using three-valued modal logic and semitopologies Volume: NN Journal: Electronic Notes in Theoretical Informatics and Computer Science Volume: NN
Murdoch J. Gabbay

Abstract
We demonstrate how to formally specify distributed algorithms as declarative axiomatic theories in a modal logic. We exhibit the method on a simple voting protocol, a simple broadcast protocol, and a simple agreement protocol. The methods scale well and have been used to find errors in a proposed industrial protocol. The key novelty is to use modal logic to capture a declarative, high-level representation of essential system properties – the logical essence of the algorithm – while abstracting away from transitions of an abstract machine that implements it. It is like the difference between specifying code in a functional or logic programming language, versus specifying code in an imperative one. A logical axiomatisation in the style we propose provides a precise, compact, human-readable specification that abstractly captures essential system properties, while eliding low-level implementation details; it is more precise than a natural language description, yet more abstract than source code or a logical specification thereof. This creates new opportunities for reasoning about correctness, resilience, and failure, and could serve as a foundation for human- and machine verification efforts, design improvements, and even alternative protocol implementations.



keywords

Distributed algorithms, three-valued modal logic, semitopology

1
Introduction

1.1
Introducing an axiomatic approach

We offer a new approach to analysing distributed algorithms, by exhibiting them as axiomatic theories in a purpose-built three-valued modal logic (one with a ‘middle’ truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
in-between
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
) over a semitopology (like topology but without the condition that the intersection of two open sets is necessarily open)
[
Gab24
,
Gab25
]
.
This captures a declarative essence of the essential system properties.

Nonempty open sets of a semitopology represent quorums.
If a predicate returns
{\mathbf{t}}
(true) or
{\mathbf{f}}
(false) this reflects correct behaviour, and if it returns
{\mathbf{b}}
(both / byzantine) this reflects faulty behaviour.

The effect is similar to the difference between an explicit for-loop to apply a function
f
to each element of a list
l
, and just writing
map f l
: we abstract declaratively to the essential logic of the algorithm.

This paper is designed to introduce the basic ideas of this new declarative / axiomatic approach.
These techniques can be applied to larger examples, including modern industrial-scale ones, but here we consider simpler algorithms so that we get a clearer view of the basic idea.
We consider Bracha Broadcast and Crusader Agreement: these are short but non-trivial examples of the genre; they illustrate our techniques; and this provides an accessible introduction to and exposition on this new way to apply logic to study distributed algorithms.

The techniques presented in this paper scale well.
In fact, more complex algorithms create
more
scope to benefit from the abstractions in this paper by eliding implementational detail.
A longer draft (currently unpublished) applies similar ideas to Paxos
[
GZ25a
]
.
1
1

1

…but not identical; the logic and models are different, reflecting that Paxos is a different algorithm.

In ongoing work, we have used the techniques of this paper to find errors in a proposed industrial protocol (
Heterogeneous Paxos

[
SWvRM20
,
SWvRM21
]
), and we are using them to help design its replacement.

A note on terminology:
we will use ‘distributed algorithm’ and ‘distributed protocol’ synonymously.
The difference is in emphasis: if we write ‘algorithm’ we imply an omniscient view of the system from the outside, whereas if we write ‘protocol’ we imply a view of the system from a participant communicating with other participants according to the rules of the algorithm.
However, this difference in emphasis just reflects a switch in point of view.
The reader can safely identify both words with the same meaning throughout.

1.2
Motivation: distributed algorithms

Distributed algorithms are algorithms that run across multiple participants, such that the overall behaviour of the system emerges from their cooperation.
Contrast with a
parallel
algorithm, which also runs across multiple participants but which would still make sense running (albeit more slowly) on a single participant.
2
2

2

A GPU rendering algorithm is a good example of a typical parallel algorithm. It could run on a single-threaded CPU, and that would make perfectly good sense; it would just be too slow.
In contrast, a blockchain is a good example of an algorithm that is fundamentally distributed and not parallel: it could still run on just one single participant, but that would completely defeat the point of the blockchain, which is precisely to
be
distributed across many participants.

Distribution can provide benefits in resilience and scalability which could not be obtained any other way.

A fundamental challenge of distribution is that we cannot assume that all participants in the algorithm are correct: we need to be resilient against failure, and against hostile (also called
byzantine
) behaviour.
Distributed algorithms are notoriously difficult to design, specify, reason about, and validate, because of the inherent difficulty of coordinating multiple participants who may be faulty, hostile, drifting in and out of network connectivity
[
PS17
,
SW89
]
, and have different local clocks, states, agendas, etc.

This gives the field of distributed algorithms a very particular flavour.
Everything revolves around this central question: how does our algorithm guarantee prompt and reliable results for correct participants, when parts of the system may be faulty?

Blockchain consensus algorithms are a good source of examples, both for distributed algorithms and for how things can go wrong.
3
3

3

This work was motivated by analysis of blockchain systems.

Solana (
https://solana.com
) has experienced instances where its consensus mechanism contributed to temporary network halts
[
Yak18
,
Sho22
]
; Ripple’s (
https://ripple.com
) XRP consensus protocol has been analysed for conditions in which safety and liveness may not be guaranteed
[
ACM20
]
;
and Avalanche’s (
https://www.avax.network
) consensus protocol has faced scrutiny regarding its assumptions and performance in adversarial scenarios
[
ACT22
]
.
Even mature blockchains like Ethereum are prone to this: e.g.
balancing attacks

[
NTT21
]
and
ex-ante reorganisation
(
reorg
) attacks
[
SNM+22
]
exploit vulnerabilities in Ethereum’s current protocol.
Even as this paper was prepared, XRP consensus experienced a slowdown and stoppage, which was addressed in a GitHub commit.
4
4

4

cryptodamus.io/en/articles/news/xrpl-meltdown-64-minute-outage-xrp-ledger-s-february-4th-2025-halt-decoded
(
permalink
) and
github.com/XRPLF/rippled/pull/5277
(
permalink
).

Thus, subtle flaws and/or ambiguities in protocol design have significant consequences, emphasising the need for rigorous analysis and well-defined specifications in this complex and safety-critical field.

Meanwhile in the real world, such analysis and specification may be abbreviated or skipped altogether: that is, in industrial practice a protocol may go from an initial idea written in English or pseudocode to an implementation, without undergoing a rigorous analysis.
This is because such analysis is would be just too expensive.
Part of the motivation for this work is, via logic, to provide a precise, compact, nontrivial, yet (relatively) lightweight methodology for specifying and reasoning about distributed protocols.
Returning to the example above of an explicit for-loop versus a map function, the idea is to attain compact proofs by being as declarative and static as possible, while preserving the essence of the algorithm.

Map of the paper

•

2
.
This gives us an opportunity to introduce semitopologies and three-valued logic.

•

3
: this is the formal language which we will use for our axioms and correctness properties.

•

4
.

•

We study Crusader Agreement in Section
5
.

•

Finally, we conclude with Section
6
.

2
Motivation and an illustrative example: voting

We will warm up by studying a voting algorithm.
This is simpler than Bracha Broadcast but is still enough to invoke much of our machinery, including the three-valued logic and semitopologies.
5
5

5

We defer a treatment of quantifiers and formal logical syntax to the case of Bracha Broadcast.

The axiomatisation is in Figure
2
.
In Example
2.1
we describe the protocol informally:

2.1
Protocol description

Example 2.1
.

Fix
f\geq 1
and consider a set
\mathsf{P}
of
3f{+}1

participants
.
Call a subset
Q\subseteq\mathsf{P}
a
quorum
when
\#Q\geq 2f{+}1
; call it a
coquorum
when
\#Q\leq f
; and call it a
contraquorum
or
blocking set
when
\#Q\geq f{+}1
.
A protocol to hold a ballot to choose between
true

{\mathbf{t}}
and
false

{\mathbf{f}}
, proceeds as follows:

(1)

Each participant
p

broadcasts
to all participants a
vote
message carrying a payload
{\mathbf{f}}
or
{\mathbf{t}}
.

(2)

If
p
receives vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
messages from a quorum of participants, then
p
deems that the ballot has succeeded and
p

observes

{\mathbf{t}}
.
Similarly for
{\mathbf{f}}
.

If no such majority is observed, then the ballot is deemed to have failed and
p
observes no value.

Remark 2.2
.

We need to choose our failure assumptions:

•

do messages always arrive;

•

do participants crash;

•

do they follow the algorithm, or could some be dishonest/faulty/byzantine/hostile (= not follow the algorithm)?

There is no right or wrong here: we just need to be clear about our assumptions.
6
6

6

For convenient reference, I include here a brief survey of some of the terminology used in the distributed systems and blockchain literature.
Call a participant in a distributed algorithm
correct
or
honest
when they follow the protocol of the algorithm.
These are synonymous, but the former suggests that we view the participant as a machine, whereas the latter suggests a participant with agency to choose whether to misbehave (e.g. in a blockchain system).
Call a participant that does not follow the protocol
faulty
(for machines) or
byzantine
or
adversarial
or
hostile
(for participants with agency).
Types of fault include
crash fault
(stops sending messages),
byzantine fault
(may deviate arbitrarily from the protocol), or
omission fault
(observes the protocol when live, but might choose to fake a crash).
In real life, it is also possible to play timing games (e.g. acting at the last possible moment).
These are all ‘honest’ in a technical sense, though not necessarily in a social or legal sense; e.g.
front running
in trading is illegal. That is out of scope for this paper.

For this exercise we assume that: message-passing is reliable (messages do not get lost or delayed); participants never crash; participants always send messages when they should; however, some participants may be dishonest and send vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
messages to some participants and vote-
{\mathbf{f}}
messages to others, contrary to the protocol which specifies they should broadcast the
same
vote value to
all
participants.

This might lead to a situation in which honest participants disagree about which vote won the ballot.
We want to make sure that this is impossible; or to be more precise, we want to know what conditions are sufficient to ensure Agreement (this property is also called Consistency):
all honest participants observe the same value, if they observe any value at all
.

We can prove Agreement under an assumption that there is a quorum
Q
of honest participants,
i.e. that there are at most
f
dishonest participants:
Observe by a counting argument that any two quorums
Q_{1},Q_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{P}
of votes (each of which contains at least
2f{+}1
participants) must intersect
Q
on at least one participant
q\in Q\cap Q_{1}\cap Q_{2}
.
Since
q\in Q
, it is honest.
This means it is impossible for a participant
p
to observe
\#Q_{1}\geq 2f{+}1
vote messages for
{\mathbf{t}}
, while at the same time
another
participant
p^{\prime}
observes
\#Q_{2}\geq 2f{+}1
vote messages for
{\mathbf{f}}
, because one participant is honest and votes either for
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
.

We now show how to declaratively model the protocol and the proof of Agreement.
First, we will need two ingredients: a notion of
truth
, which we define in Subsection
2.2
; and a notion of
quorum
, which we define in Subsection
2.3
.

2.2
Three-valued logic

\hskip-10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c c c}p\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c c c}p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c c c}p\mathbin{\rightarrow}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c c c}p\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c c c}p\equiv q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[34.44434pt]
\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{-0pt}}c}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\hfil\hskip 0.\\
{\mathbf{t}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{-0pt}}c}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5ptp\hfil\hskip 0.\\
{\mathbf{t}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{-0pt}}c}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5ptp\hfil\hskip 0.\\
{\mathbf{t}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{-0pt}}c}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5ptp\hfil\hskip 0.\\
{\mathbf{t}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\hspace{-0pt}}c}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5ptp\hfil\hskip 0.\\
{\mathbf{t}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}\hfil\hskip 0.&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c c c}p\oplus q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Vertical axis above indicates values of
p
; horizontal axis (if nontrivial) indicates values of
q
.

Figure 1:
Truth-tables for some operations on
{\mathbf{3}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
(Definition
1
)

Our notion of truth will be three-valued:

Definition 1
.

(1)

Let
{\mathbf{3}}=({\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}})
be a lattice of
truth-values
with elements

•

{\mathbf{f}}
(false), which is less than

•

{\mathbf{b}}
(Both/Byzantine/Broken/amBivalent), which is less than

•

{\mathbf{t}}
(true).

Define a unary connective
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
(
negation
), and binary connectives
\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
(
conjunction
),
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
(
disjunction
),
\mathbin{\rightarrow}
(
weak implication
),
\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}
(
strong implication
),

\equiv

(
identity
), and
\oplus
(
exclusive-or
),
and define modalities
\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt
,
\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt
,
\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt
,
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt
, as in Figure
1
.

Note that these connectives are not minimal, e.g.
\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
is equal to
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}=\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
.

(2)

Suppose
X
is a set.
We may lift connectives from
{\mathbf{3}}
to the function-space
X\to{\mathbf{3}}
in the natural way.
Thus: if
f,f^{\prime}:X\to{\mathbf{3}}
then we may write

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f=\lambda\mathit{tv}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f(\mathit{tv})\quad\text{and}\quad f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime}=\lambda\mathit{tv}.f(\mathit{tv})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime}(\mathit{tv})\quad\text{and}\quad f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}f^{\prime}=\lambda\mathit{tv}.f(\mathit{tv})\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}f^{\prime}(\mathit{tv}).

(3)

If
\mathit{tv}\in{\mathbf{3}}
then write
\vDash\mathit{tv}
for the judgement ‘
\mathit{tv}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
’ and read this as
\mathit{tv}
is
valid
.
7
7

7

There is a standard distinction in logic between being a truth-value being
true
(which means: it is equal to
{\mathbf{t}}
) and a logical judgement being
valid
(which may mean ‘a true assertion about a model’ or ‘it is derivable in some derivation system’ or ‘it evaluates to true in a given context’).
In two-valued logic this distinction is less prominent, because a predicate may be judged to be valid when it evaluates to
{\mathbf{t}}
(possibly in the context of some valuation and/or interpretation). But here, we have three truth-values, and a predicate will be judged to be valid when it evaluates to
{\mathbf{t}}

or

{\mathbf{b}}
.

Write
\mathrel{\not\vDash}\mathit{tv}
for ‘
\mathit{tv}={\mathbf{f}}
’ (this happens precisely when
\neg(\vDash\mathit{tv})
) and read this as
\mathit{tv}
is
invalid
or is
false
.

(4)

We may write
\equiv
for equality on truth-values, to distinguish this (where this might be helpful for clarity) from a later notion of equality on values.
8
8

8

A binary connective
{\equiv}:{\mathbf{3}}\to{\mathbf{3}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is presented as a truth-table in Figure
1
– meaning that we overload
\equiv
-the-identity-judgement with
\equiv
-the-function-on-truth-values.
This is defensible because it is true that
\mathit{tv}
and
\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
are identical, precisely when
\mathit{tv}\equiv\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
, and it is false when
\equiv
returns
{\mathbf{f}}
.
In symbols:
((\mathit{tv}\equiv\mathit{tv}^{\prime})\equiv{\mathbf{t}})\equiv(\mathit{tv}\equiv\mathit{tv}^{\prime})
.

The implicational structure of
{\mathbf{3}}
is rich (it supports sixteen implications
[
AAZ11
, note 5, page 22]
).
For us, the weak implication
\mathbin{\rightarrow}
and strong implication
\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}
will suffice, and we have:

Proposition 2
.

For truth-values
\mathit{tv},\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
, we have:

(1)

\vDash{\mathbf{t}}
and
\vDash{\mathbf{b}}
and
\mathrel{\not\vDash}{\mathbf{f}}
.

(2)

\vDash\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
if and only if
{\vDash\mathit{tv}}\lor{\vDash\mathit{tv}^{\prime}}
, and
\vDash\mathit{tv}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
if and only if
{\vDash\mathit{tv}}\land{\vDash\mathit{tv}^{\prime}}
.

(3)

(\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})\equiv({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})
and

(\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})\equiv(\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}^{\prime})
.

(4)

\vDash\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{tv}\equiv{\mathbf{t}}
implies
\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
We call this
weak modus ponens
.

(5)

\vDash\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{tv}\equiv{\mathbf{t}}
implies
\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\equiv{\mathbf{t}}
.
We call this
strong modus ponens
.

(6)

\vDash\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}
.
In jargon, validity satisfies
excluded middle
(
p
-or-not-
p
is valid).

(7)

\vDash\mathit{tv}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}
if and only if
\mathit{tv}={\mathbf{b}}
if and only if
\vDash\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}
.
9
9

9

This means that our notion of validity is
paraconsistent
, so
\phi
and not-
\phi
may both be valid (
[
AAZ11
]
, or see the nice survey in
[
Pri02
]
). Note that in our setting
\phi
and not-
\phi
cannot not be simultaneously
true
, since
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{t}}={\mathbf{f}}
.

(8)

\vDash\mathit{tv}
if and only if
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}\equiv\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}
.

(9)

\mathit{tv}\leq\mathit{tv}^{\prime}
if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\leq{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}
.

Proof 2.3
.

By inspection of the truth-tables in Figure
1
.

2.3
Semitopologies

\begin{array}[]{r@{\quad}l@{\qquad}r@{\quad}l}{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}&\mathit{observe}(p)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}&{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}?)}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{observe}(p)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}\\
{\mathsf{(Observe!)}}&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\mathit{observe}(p)&{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}!)}}&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{observe}(p)\\
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\circ\mathit{vote})&{\mathsf{(3twined)}}&(\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime})\leq{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})\end{array}

Above:
\circ
denotes function composition;
f
and
f^{\prime}
range over arbitrary functions in
\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
and
p
ranges over elements of
\mathsf{P}
.

\leq
refers to the lattice ordering on
{\mathbf{3}}
, namely
{\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}}
.

\textstyle\mathop{\square}

\textstyle\cdot

and

\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}

\textstyle\cdot

have type
(\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}})\to{\mathbf{3}}
here, which is why
\mathit{oberve}
is applied to
p
above and
\mathit{vote}
is not.

\mathit{vote}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
means ‘
p
voted for
{\mathbf{t}}
’;
\mathit{vote}(p)={\mathbf{f}}
means ‘
p
voted for
{\mathbf{f}}
’ and
\mathit{vote}(p)={\mathbf{b}}
means ‘
p
sent conficted messages about its vote’.
Similarly for
\mathit{observe}
.

Figure 2:
A simple voting protocol (Definition
6
)

We define our universe of participants.
This has a topological flavour:

Definition 3
.

A
semitopology

[
Gab24
,
Gab25
]
is a pair
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
of

•

a set of
points

\mathsf{P}
– which we may call
participants
, because we may think of them as participants running a distributed algorithm – and

•

open sets

\mathsf{Open}\subseteq\mathit{powerset}(\mathsf{P})
, such that
\mathsf{P}\in\mathsf{Open}
and
\mathsf{Open}
is closed under arbitrary (possibly empty) unions.

Write
\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid O\neq\varnothing\}
.
We may call
P\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
a
quorum
.

A semitopology naturally gives rise to a modal logical structure, as follows:

Definition 4
.

Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a function, which we can think of as a unary predicate (a three-valued one, taking truth-values in
{\mathbf{3}}
).
Define functions

\Box\hskip 1.5pt,\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt,\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$},{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}:(\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}})\to{\mathbf{3}}

— where we read

\Box\hskip 1.5ptf
as ‘
everywhere
-
f
’,
\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf
as ‘
somewhere
-
f
’,

\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f
as ‘
quorum
-
f
’, and
{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f
as ‘
contraquorum
-
f
’
— by

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}r@{\ }l@{\qquad}r@{\ }l@{\qquad}r@{\ }l}\Box\hskip 1.5ptf\equiv&\bigwedge_{p\in\mathsf{P}}f(p)&\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf\equiv&\bigvee_{p\in\mathsf{P}}f(p)\\
\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\equiv&\bigvee_{O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}}\bigwedge_{p\in O}f(p)&{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f\equiv&\bigwedge_{O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}}\bigvee_{p\in O}f(p).\end{array}

Lemma 2.4
.

We note some
de Morgan
(for parts
1
and
2
) and
contrapositive
(for part
3
)
dualities:

(1)

Suppose
X
is a set and
f,f^{\prime}:X\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then we have:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}r@{\ }l}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})\equiv&({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f^{\prime})&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}f^{\prime})\equiv&({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f^{\prime})\\
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f)\equiv&\Box\hskip 1.5ptf&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box\hskip 1.5pt({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f)\equiv&\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf\\
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f)\equiv&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f)\equiv&{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f\end{array}

(2)

Suppose
\mathit{tv}\in{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}\equiv\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}\equiv\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}
.

(3)

Suppose
\mathit{tv},\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then
(\mathit{tv}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})\equiv(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{tv})
.

Proof 2.5
.

By routine arguments on lattices and truth-tables.

Remark 2.6
.

Semitopologies
[
Gab24
,
Gab25
]
were designed to generalise the quorum systems like Example
2.1
(expositions are in
[
MR98
,
ACTZ24
]
):

quorum
corresponds to
nonempty open set
;
coquorum
(the sets complement to a quorum) corresponds to
non-total closed set
; and
contraquorum
or
blocking set
(a set that intersects every quorum) corresponds to
dense set
.

There are some nice benefits to working with a semitopology as per Definition
3
, rather than with a concrete structure as per Example
2.1
:

(1)

‘
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
’ is shorter, and more mathematically rich, than ‘a set
O
having
2f{+}1
elements’.

(2)

It notes something that is arguably obvious in retrospect, but which does not appear to have been noted before semitopologies: that things typically done in the distributed systems literature are topological in nature.

(3)

As Definition
4
and Lemma
2.4
suggest, and the rest of this paper substantiates, we can exploit the connection between topology and logic to reason axiomatically on distributed algorithms.

Remark 2.7
.

\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt
and
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt
commute with all monotone connectives: for
\mathsf{M}\in\{\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt,\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\}
and
\mathit{tv},\mathit{tv}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
we have
\mathsf{M}(\mathit{tv}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})\equiv((\mathsf{M}\mathit{tv})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{M}\mathit{tv}^{\prime})
and similarly for
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
,
\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt
,
\Box\hskip 1.5pt
,

\textstyle\mathop{\square}

\textstyle\cdot

, and

\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}

\textstyle\cdot

.

The reader can also easily check facts like
\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}=\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}=\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}=\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{tv}
, and so on.

We may rewrite using these properties without comment henceforth.

We warm up with an easy lemma that connects
\Box\hskip 1.5pt
with

\textstyle\mathop{\square}

\textstyle\cdot

:

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then

(\Box\hskip 1.5ptf\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime})\leq\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime}).

(Recall that
{\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}}
in
{\mathbf{3}}
as a lattice. The
\leq
above refers to this lattice ordering.)

As a corollary,
\vDash\Box\hskip 1.5pt(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt{\circ}f^{\prime})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime}
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime}
.

Proof 2.9
.

By routine lattice calculations from Definition
4
.
Or, using topological language: if every
p\in\mathsf{P}
satisfies
f(p)
, and there exists
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that every
p\in O^{\prime}
satisfies
f^{\prime}(p)
, then clearly every
p\in O^{\prime}
satisfies
f(p)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime}(p)
.
The corollary follows taking
f=\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\circ f^{\prime}
.

A characteristic fact connecting

\textstyle\mathop{\square}

\textstyle\cdot

,

\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}

\textstyle\cdot

, and
\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt
is this:

Lemma 2.10
.

Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then

(\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime})\leq\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime}).

As corollaries:

(1)

\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime}
implies
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})
.

(2)

\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime}
implies
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf^{\prime}
.

(3)

\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\circ f^{\prime})
and
\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime}
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf^{\prime}
(equivalently:
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5ptf^{\prime}
).

Proof 2.11
.

By routine lattice calculations from Definition
4
.
Or, using topological language:
if we have an open set of
f
s and a dense set of
f^{\prime}
s, then there must be some point with both
f
and
f^{\prime}
.
Part
1
follows; part
2
follows from part
1
setting
f=\lambda p.{\boldsymbol{\top}}
; and part
3
follows from part
1
taking
f=\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\circ f^{\prime}
.

2.4
3-twined semitopologies

Definition 5
.

For
n\geq 0
, call a semitopology
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})

n
-twined
when any
n
nonempty open sets have a nonempty intersection
[
GZ25b
, Section 5]
.
That is: if
O_{1},\dots,O_{n}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
then
\bigcap_{1\leq i\leq n}O_{i}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
.

In this paper we will care most about
3
-twined semitopologies; semitopologies such that any three nonempty open sets have a nonempty intersection.

Remark 2.12
.

Indexing the open sets from
1
in Definition
5
even though we only insist that
n\geq 0
, is not a typo.
By convention,
\bigcap\varnothing=\mathsf{P}
, so a semitopology is
0
-twined just when
\mathsf{P}
is nonempty.
The reader can also check that every semitopology is
1
-twined.

So the
n
-twined condition starts getting more structured from
2
-twined onwards.

The terminology ‘3-twined’ follows terminology from the semitopologies literature: it generalises the notion of
intertwined
space considered in
[
Gab24
,
Gab25
]
(intertwined =
2
-twined) and see also the Section on
n
-twined semitopologies in
[
GZ25b
]
.
However, the general concept is well-known in the literature, albeit not phrased systematically and in (semi)topological terms.
For example, being 3-twined is called the
Q^{3}
property in
[
ACTZ24
, Definition 2]
.

The semitopology in Example
2.1
is the canonical example of a 3-twined semitopology, though other examples are easy to generate, e.g. we can take
4f{+}1
participants with quorums sets of size at least
3f{+}1
.

The fundamental logical property of a 3-twined semitopology is this:

Theorem 2.13
.

Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is 3-twined and
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{P}{\to}{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then
(\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime})\leq{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})
.
10
10

10

The reverse of this result also holds: if for every
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
we have
(\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime})\leq{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})
, then
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is 3-twined.

As a corollary
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime}
implies
\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})
.

Proof 2.14
.

\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime}={\mathbf{t}}
implies that there exist
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
on which
f
and
f^{\prime}
respectively take the value
{\mathbf{t}}
.
By the 3-twined property,
O\cap O^{\prime}
intersects every other
O^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
, thus
{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})={\mathbf{t}}
.

\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime}={\mathbf{b}}
implies that there exist
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
on which
f
and
f^{\prime}
respectively take values in
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
By the 3-twined property
O\cap O^{\prime}
intersects every other
O^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\hskip-1.0pt{\neq}\varnothing}
, thus
{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

Corollary
2.15
is a (rather elegant) dual rephrasing of Theorem
2.13
and will be useful later:

Corollary 2.15
.

Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is 3-twined and
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
are functions.
Then:

(1)

\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}(f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}f^{\prime})\leq({\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime})
.

(2)

As a corollary,
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}(f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}f^{\prime})
implies
\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime}
.

Proof 2.16
.

By Theorem
2.13

(\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f^{\prime})\leq{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}f^{\prime})
