Remark 2.17
.

It might be helpful to sum up some highlights of the maths so far.
Suppose
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f,f^{\prime}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

Suppose further that
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f
, meaning that
f
is correct (=
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
, not
{\mathbf{b}}
) on a quorum (= nonempty open set) of participants.
This is a typical failure assumption as found in the distributed systems literature.

Then we have seen that:

(1)

\vDash\Box\hskip 1.5ptf
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f
.

(2)

\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f
.

(3)

\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf
.

(4)

\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\circ}g)
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5ptf
.

(5)

If
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is
3
-twined then
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}f^{\prime}
implies
\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(f\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f^{\prime})
and
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}(f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}f^{\prime})
implies
\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}f^{\prime}
.

2.5
A simple voting protocol

We are now ready to consider our first (very simple) protcol:

Definition 6
.

Call a tuple
\mu=((\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open}),\mathit{vote},\mathit{observe})
, where

•

(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is a(n arbitrary) semitopology and

•

\mathit{vote},\mathit{observe}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
are functions,

a
model
.
We call a model
\mu
a
model
(of
ThyVote
) when the axioms in Figure
2
are valid in
\mu
.

Remark 2.18
.

We take a minute to unpack what the notation in Figure
2
is intended to mean:

(1)

\mathit{vote}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
indicates that
p
voted for
{\mathbf{t}}
.

\mathit{vote}(p)={\mathbf{f}}
indicates that
p
voted for
{\mathbf{f}}
.

\mathit{vote}(p)={\mathbf{b}}
indicates that
p
(is hostile because it) sent vote messages for
both

{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
.
11
11

11

There is no notion here of ‘not voted yet’, because there is no notion of time!

\mathit{vote}
just records the votes that a participant casts, and our failure assumptions assume that
some
vote is made, as per Remark
2.2
.
Different assumptions would yield different axioms.

(2)

\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
indicates that
p
observed a quorum of vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
messages – some of which may be from hostile participants;
p
cannot know.
Similarly for
\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{f}}
.

\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{b}}
indicates that
p
observed no quorum of messages either way.

Remark 2.19
.

We discuss how the axioms in Figure
2
correspond to the protocol in Example
2.1
:

(1)

{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}
is a
backward rule
: it express that
if
a participant
p
observes
{\mathbf{t}}
, then it must have received a quorum of vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
messages.

We use weak implication
\mathbin{\rightarrow}
(not strong implication
\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}
) in
{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}
because a quorum may include some dishonest (hostile) participants who sent dishonest messages; i.e. vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
to some participants and vote-
{\mathbf{f}}
to others.
We represent the combination of vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
and vote-
{\mathbf{f}}
messages from a hostile participant
q
by setting
\mathit{vote}(q)={\mathbf{b}}
.
Thus, if
\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
it does not follows that
\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}={\mathbf{t}}
; it only follows that
\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
This highlights that
\mathit{vote}(p)
in our declarative model is a single
truth-value
, not a
message
or a set of messages.

(2)

{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}?)}}
just repeats
{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}
for the case that
p
receives a quorum of vote-
{\mathbf{f}}
.

(3)

{\mathsf{(Observe!)}}
/
{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}!)}}
are
forward rules
: they express that if an honest quorum votes for
{\mathbf{t}}
/
{\mathbf{f}}
, then every
p
observes
{\mathbf{t}}
/
{\mathbf{f}}
.

Why did we use strong implication here, not weak implication?
We could use either, but we make a design choice: since (in our protocol) participants do not report their
\mathit{observe}
to anyone else, there is no useful sense in which lying would be meaningful.
So we use the strong implication.

(4)

{\mathsf{(Correct)}}
asserts that some quorum of participants are honest: they vote
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
, and not
{\mathbf{b}}
.

(5)

{\mathsf{(3twined)}}
expresses the characteristic intersection property of a 3-twined semitopology, as per Theorem
2.13
.
For this protocol we will use it once, setting
f=\mathit{vote}
and
f^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}
.

Remark 2.20
.

At the level of
\mathit{vote}
,
{\mathbf{b}}
denotes a hostile participant sending mixed votes to different participants.
At the level of
\mathit{observe}
,
{\mathbf{b}}
just indicates that a participant has observed no quorum of participants voting for
{\mathbf{t}}
or voting for
{\mathbf{f}}
.

This use of
{\mathbf{b}}
to mean two things, depending on the context, is a feature, not a bug.
It illustrates a fact about truth-values in
{\mathbf{3}}
: they are just data, which we -- via our choice of axioms and intended interpretation -- can interpret as we choose and as is mathematically convenient.
12
12

12

An example from real life. If my wife asks me “You didn’t forget to put the rubbish out, did you?” and I return a value “Yes”, does this mean “Yes, I did forget!” or “Yes, I did put the rubbish out!”? The “Yes” here is raw data; its meaning exists in the (hopefully shared) understanding of the people having the conversation.
In the same way,
{\mathbf{b}}
is just data, and is meaningless until we –
we
– use it in axioms and interpret those axioms.

Note in Figure
2
that
{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}
has a dual
{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}?)}}
, and
{\mathsf{(Observe!)}}
has a dual
{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}!)}}
– but
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}
has no dual axiom
{\mathsf{(Correct{\boldsymbol{\neg}})}}
.
Why is this so?
Because
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}
is self-dual:

Lemma 2.21
.

(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}\mathit{vote})\equiv(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote})
.

Proof 2.22
.

We sketch an argument that just relies on dualities and some basic symbol-chasing.
By part
3
of Lemma
2.4
,

(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}\mathit{vote})\equiv(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote})
.
Using parts
1
and
2
of the same result, we simplify the right-hand side to
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}
.

Lemma 2.23
.

\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}
,
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}
,
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{observe}\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}
and

\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightharpoonup}\Box\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{observe}
.

Proof 2.24
.

The reasoning is very easy and we prove just the first two parts.

(1)

By weak modus ponens (Prop.
2
(
4
)) it suffices to show that
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}
implies
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}
.
So suppose
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}
.
Using the meaning of
\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt
from Definition
4
, there exists
p\in\mathsf{P}
such that
\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
, and so
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}(p)
.
Then using weak modus ponens and
{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}
we have
\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}
.

(2)

By strong modus ponens (Prop.
2
(
5
)) and the meaning of
\Box\hskip 1.5pt
, it suffices to show that
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}(p)
for every
p\in\mathsf{P}
.
But this is immediate from
{\mathsf{(Observe!)}}
.

We can now prove it is impossible for one participant to observe
{\mathbf{t}}
and another to observe
{\mathbf{f}}
:

Proposition 7

(Agreement)
.

If
\mu
is a model of
ThyVote
then

\mathrel{\not\vDash}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{observe}
.

Proof 2.25
.

Suppose
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{observe}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{observe}
.
Then there exist
p,p^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}
such that
\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
and
\mathit{observe}(p^{\prime})={\mathbf{f}}
.
Using
{\mathsf{(Observe?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}?)}}

\vDash\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote}
.
By
{\mathsf{(3twined)}}

\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}(\mathit{vote}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathit{vote})
, so using
Proposition
2
(
7
)
\vDash{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{vote}
.
Using
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}
and Lemma
2.10
(
1
)
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt(\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{vote}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{vote})
.
But
(\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{vote}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathit{vote})\equiv{\mathbf{f}}
, and
\vDash\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt{\mathbf{f}}
is impossible.

Remark 2.26
.

A more traditional proof for Proposition
7
would work concretely with the set of quorums from Example
2.1
, and prove Agreement by counting and calculations (see for example Lemmas 1 to 4 used in the proof of Theorem 1 from
[
Bra87
, page 135]
).

Proposition
7
is not that proof: the semitopology is abstract, except for satisfying the axioms in Figure
2
.
The reasoning is logical in flavour and follows the structure of the axioms.
This is simple, general, and it captures the logical essence of what the concrete counting proofs are actually doing.

Remark 2.27
.

The failure model is encoded in the axioms – typically this shows up in the strength of the implications we use.
For example: our failure model in Remark
2.2
assumed a reliable network, which is reflected by the use of
strong implication
in the forward rules
{\mathsf{(Observe!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}!)}}
in Figure
2
.

To model an unreliable network, in which messages sent might not arrive, we would weaken
{\mathsf{(Observe!)}}
to
\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathit{vote}\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathit{observe}
, and similarly for
{\mathsf{(Observe{\boldsymbol{\neg}}!)}}
.
This reflects that a quorum of honest participants vote for
{\mathbf{t}}
, but the network fails to transmit their messages so a participant
p
(even if honest) may remain unable to observe and so set
\mathit{observe}(p)={\mathbf{b}}
.

3
The modal logic

The above concludes our discussion of the simple voting protocol.
We are now ready to start studying Bracha Broadcast.
We will need two more ingredients:

(1)

Our voting example was binary (vote-
{\mathbf{t}}
or vote-
{\mathbf{f}}
).
For Bracha Broadcast we assume an arbitrary nonempty set of values
\mathsf{Value}
, over which we will need three-valued quantifiers like unique existence
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}:\mathsf{Value}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
We study this in Subsection
3.1
.

(2)

Our voting example worked directly with semantics; we made assertions directly about functions like
\mathit{vote}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
For Bracha Broadcast it is better to build the syntax and semantics of a (simple) logic; we will have predicate-symbols like
\mathsf{echo}
, which will be interpreted as functions like
\varsigma(\mathsf{echo}):\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{Value}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
We study this in Subsection
3.2
.

3.1
(Unique/affine) existence in three-valued logic

Distributed algorithms often require an implementation to make choices, e.g. ‘respond only to the first message you receive’.
In axioms, we model this using unique existence and affine existence:

Definition 8
.

Suppose
\mathsf{Value}
is a set and
f:\mathsf{Value}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a function.
Define
existence

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
,
affine existence

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
(there exist zero or one) and
unique existence

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
(there exists precisely one) by:
13
13

13

The standard symbol for unique existence is
\exists!
. I am not aware of a standard symbol for affine existence. I use
\exists_{1}
and
\exists_{01}
for clarity and consistency.

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f=\bigvee_{v\in\mathsf{Value}}f(v)\quad{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f=\bigwedge_{v,v^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Value}}(f(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f(v^{\prime}))\mathbin{\rightarrow}v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}\quad{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f=({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f).

Remark 3.1
.

Note that

\exists

,

\exists_{1}

, and

\exists_{01}

return truth-values in
{\mathbf{3}}
, whereas the usual Boolean quantifiers – write them
\exists
,
\exists_{1}
, and
\exists_{01}
– return truth-values in
\{\bot,\top\}
.
So even if ‘

\exists_{01}

’ looks like ‘
\exists_{01}
’, these are different creatures.

Therefore, continuing the notation of Definition
8
, we spell out cases to check how the Definition works:
Then:

(1)

If
\exists v\neq v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}=f(v^{\prime})
, then

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{f}}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(2)

If
\exists_{1}v\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}
, and
\forall v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v^{\prime})\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
, then

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{t}}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(3)

If
\exists_{1}v\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}
, and
\exists v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v^{\prime})={\mathbf{b}}
, then

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{b}}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(4)

If
\neg\exists v\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}
and
\exists_{1}v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v^{\prime})={\mathbf{b}}
, then

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{b}}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

If there is only one non-
{\mathbf{f}}
value, then arguably
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
should be
{\mathbf{t}}
(not
{\mathbf{b}}
).
In practice, this will not matter: we will only use Proposition
9
and that is unaffected either way.

(5)

If
\neg\exists v\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}
and
\exists v,v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Value}.v\neq v^{\prime}\land f(v)={\mathbf{b}}=f(v^{\prime})
, then

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{b}}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

If
\forall v\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{b}}
, then
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{b}}
– note that this is
not

{\mathbf{f}}
.
This illustrates that in our three-valued setting, ‘unique existence’ is not the same as ‘unique non-false value’.

(6)

If
\forall v\in\mathsf{Value}.f(v)={\mathbf{f}}
, then

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{f}}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f={\mathbf{t}}
.

Our proofs will use unique/affine existence via Proposition
9
:

Proposition 9
.

Suppose
V
is a set and
f:V\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a function and
v,v^{\prime}\in V
.
Then:

(1)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(b)

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

(c)

\exists_{01}v{\in}V.(\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5ptf(v))
.

(d)

\exists_{01}v{\in}V.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}
.

(e)

\forall v,v^{\prime}{\in}V.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}=f(v^{\prime})\Longrightarrow v=v^{\prime}
.

(2)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(b)

\exists v{\in}V.(\vDash f(v))
and
\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(3)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(b)

(\exists_{01}v{\in}V.f(v)={\mathbf{t}})\land(\forall v{\in}V.f(v)\neq{\mathbf{b}})
.

(4)

The following are equivalent:

(a)

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
.

(b)

\exists_{1}v{\in}V.f(v)={\mathbf{t}}
and
\forall v{\in}V.f(v)\neq{\mathbf{b}}
.

(5)

If
\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
or
\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}f
and
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt(f(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}f(v^{\prime}))
then
v=v^{\prime}
.

Proof 3.2
.

We unpack Definition
8
and check the possibilities in Remark
3.1
.

A simple modal logic

Figure 3:
Syntax of a simple modal logic (Definition
10
)

Definition 10
.

We fix an infinite set of
variable symbols

a,b\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
.
We assume a nonempty set of
values

v\in\mathsf{Value}
and a set of
predicate symbols

\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Specifically:

•

For our example of Bracha Broadcast below, we set
\mathsf{Value}
to be any nonempty set and we take

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathsf{bcst},\mathsf{echo},\mathsf{ready},\mathsf{dlvr}\}
.

•

For our example of Crusader Agreement below, we set
\mathsf{Value}=\{0,{0.5},1\}
and we take

\mathcal{P}=\{\mathsf{input},\mathsf{echo}_{1},\mathsf{echo}_{2},\mathsf{output}\}
.

We then define
terms

t
,
predicates

\phi
, and syntactic sugar as in Figure
3
.

Write
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
for
substitution
of
a
for
v
; this is the result of replacing every unbound
a
in
\phi
with
v
.
We may abuse notation and write
v
for quantified variables, e.g. writing
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\mathsf{echo}(v)
instead of
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{echo}(a)
; it will always be clear what is meant.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\bot}}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&{\boldsymbol{\bot}}&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\lambda v.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\lambda v.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt({[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p))&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{P}(v)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)=&\varsigma(\mathsf{P})(p)(v)\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{$(\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P})$\hss}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l@{\qquad}l@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }l}p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi&\text{means}&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}&\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi&\text{means}&\forall p{\in}\mathsf{P}.(p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi)\\
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Phi&\text{means}&\forall\phi{\in}\Phi.(\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi)&\Phi\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi&\text{means}&\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Phi\Longrightarrow\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi\end{array}

Figure 4:
Denotation for a simple modal logic (Definition
11
)

Definition 11
.

(1)

Given a semitopology
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
, an
interpretation

\varsigma
is an assignment for each
\mathsf{P}\in\mathcal{P}
of a function
\varsigma(\mathsf{P}):\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{Value}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

(2)

A
model

\mu=(\mathsf{Value},(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open}),\varsigma)
consists of a set of values, a semitopology, and an interpretation.

(3)

Given a model
\mu
, we define a
denotation

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}:\mathsf{P}\to{\mathbf{3}}
and
validity

p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi
and
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi
as per Figure
4
.

(4)

Call an axiom
\phi

valid
in
\mu
when
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi
.
Call a set of axioms
Thy
a
theory
.

(5)

Write
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\text{Thy}
when
\forall\phi{\in}\text{Thy}.(\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi)
(i.e. the axioms of
Thy
are all valid in
\mu
), in which case we call
\mu
a
model of

Thy
.

(6)

Write
\text{Thy}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi
when
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\text{Thy}
implies
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi
(i.e. every model of
Thy
satisfies
\phi
).

{nttnn}

In what follows, we may use the fact that
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\phi
is equivalent to
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\phi
without comment, and similarly for the other modalities
\Box\hskip 1.5pt
,

\textstyle\mathop{\square}

\textstyle\cdot

, and

\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}

\textstyle\cdot

, and for compound predicates like
\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\phi\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\phi
.
This pattern will be quite common in the proofs that follow, because many of our axioms have the form
\phi\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\phi^{\prime}
, so when we use weak modus ponens (Prop.
2
(
4
)) we may assume
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\phi
and deduce
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\phi^{\prime}
(not
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\phi^{\prime}
); the
p
is still there in some sense, but it no longer matters.

4
Declarative Bracha Broadcast

4.1
Definition of the theory

Remark 4.1
.

Bracha Broadcast is a classic broadcast algorithm
[
Bra87
]
.
An English description of the algorithm is given in Figure
5
.
It is designed to allow a group of participants to agree on at most one value, in the presence of some hostile participants.

Protocol description.

(1)

A designated
sender
participant sends messages to all participants,
broadcasting
a value
v
.
Message-passing is reliable, so messages always arrive.

(2)

If a participant receives a broadcast
v
message from the sender – it is assumed that every participant knows who the sender is, and other participants cannot forge the sender’s signature – it sends an
echo

v
to all other participants.

Each participant will only echo
once
, so if it receives two broadcast messages with different values then it will only echo one of them.

(3)

If a participant receives a quorum of echo messages for a value
v
, or a contraquorum of ready messages for a value
v
, then it sends messages to all participants declaring itself
ready
with
v
.

(4)

If a participant receives a quorum of ready messages for a value
v
, then the participant
delivers

v
.

Failure assumptions.

Our failure assumption will be that a quorum of participants follow the algorithm, and a coquorum (the complement of a quorum, i.e. a nontotal closed set) of participants may not follow the algorithm (more on this terminology in Remark
2.6
).
For instance, a hostile participant may broadcast or echo multiple values
v
.

Figure 5:
Informal (English) description of Bracha Broadcast (Remark
4.1
)

\begin{array}[t]{l@{\quad}r@{\ }l}\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\lx@text@underline{\emph{Backward rules}}\hss}}\\
{\mathsf{(BrDeliver?)}}&&\mathsf{dlvr}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{ready}(a)\\
{\mathsf{(BrReady?)}}&&\mathsf{ready}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{echo}(a)\\
{\mathsf{(BrEcho?)}}&&\mathsf{echo}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(a)\\
\\
\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\lx@text@underline{\emph{Other rules}}\hss}}\\
{\mathsf{(BrEcho01)}}&&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{echo}(a)\\
{\mathsf{(BrBroadcast1)}}&&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(a)\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{l@{\quad}r@{\ }l}\text{\hbox to0.0pt{\lx@text@underline{\emph{Forward rules}}\hss}}\\
{\mathsf{(BrDeliver!)}}&&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{ready}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathsf{dlvr}(a)\\
{\mathsf{(BrReady!)}}&&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{echo}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathsf{ready}(a)\\
{\mathsf{(BrEcho!)}}&&\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{echo}(a)\\
{\mathsf{(BrReady!!)}}&&{\mathchoice{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{{\raisebox{-0.45pt}{\scalebox{1.35}[1]{$\lozenge$}}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}\mathsf{ready}(a)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathsf{ready}(a)\\
\\
{\mathsf{(BrCorrect)}}&&\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{ready}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{echo}]\\
{\mathsf{(BrCorrect^{\prime})}}&&\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{P}]\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{P}]\ (\mathsf{P}{\in}\{\mathsf{ready},\mathsf{echo}\})\\
{\mathsf{(BrCorrect^{\prime\prime})}}&&\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{bcst}]\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{bcst}]\end{array}

Free
a
above are assumed universally quantified (i.e. there is an invisible
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a
at the start of any axiom with a free
a
).
The axioms above assume predicate symbols
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathsf{bcst},\mathsf{echo},\mathsf{ready},\mathsf{dlvr}\}
, as per Definition
10
.

ThyBB
: axioms of Bracha Broadcast (Definition

)

\begin{array}[t]{l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}{\mathsf{(BrValidity?)}}&&\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)&\text{(Proposition~\ref{prop.bb.validity})}\\
{\mathsf{(BrNoDup)}}&&\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{dlvr}&\text{(Proposition~\ref{prop.no.duplication})}\\

Correctness properties for
ThyBB
(Remark
4.7
)

Definition 12
.

Define
Declarative Bracha Broadcast
to be the theory (set of axioms)
ThyBB
in Figure
6
.
Axioms are taken to be universally quantified over any free variables (there is only one:
a
).

Remark 4.2
.

A fundamental difference between a logical theory and an algorithm is:

•

With a theory, we are presented with a model and we ask “Are the axioms valid in this model?”.

•

With an algorithm, we start from nothing and we construct a model by following the algorithm.

So in this paper, we assume a model is just presented to us, and our business is to check whether it validates axioms.
All of the complexity inherent in studying an implementation – i.e. a machine to compute such a model – is elided by design.

Remark 4.3
.

As for our voting example from Figure
2
, the axioms in Figure
6
are of three kinds:

(1)

Backward rules
have names ending with
?
and represent reasoning of the form “if
this
happens, then
that
must have happened”.

(2)

Forward rules
have names ending with
!
and represent forward reasoning of the form “if
this
happens, then
that
must happen”.

(3)

Other rules
reflect particular conditions of the algorithms, e.g. having to do with failure assumptions.

Remark 4.4

(Discussion of the axioms)
.

We consider the axioms in turn.
Each is implicitly universally quantified over
a
if required; so the
a
in
{\mathsf{(BrDeliver?)}}
represents ‘any value
v\in\mathsf{Value}
’.
The truth-values
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
represent honest behaviour, and
{\mathbf{b}}
represents dishonest behaviour.
Each axiom is evaluated at an arbitrary
p\in\mathsf{P}
:

(1)

{\mathsf{(BrDeliver?)}}
says: if
\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
at
p
then for a quorum of participants
\mathsf{ready}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
.

This reflects that in the algorithm as outlined in Remark
4.1
, if
p
honestly delivers
v
then a quorum of participants must have sent ready messages for
v
, though some of those messages may have been sent by dishonest participants.

(2)

{\mathsf{(BrReady?)}}
says: if
\mathsf{ready}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
at
p
then for a quorum of participants
\mathsf{echo}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
.

(3)

{\mathsf{(BrEcho?)}}
says: if
\mathsf{echo}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
, then somebody
\mathsf{bcst}(v)
with
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
.

(4)

{\mathsf{(BrDeliver!)}}
says: if
\mathsf{ready}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
for a quorum of participants, then
\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
.

This reflects that in the algorithm, if a quorum of honest participants send ready-
v
messages and if
p
is honest, then
p
will send a deliver-
v
message.

(5)

{\mathsf{(BrReady!)}}
says: if
\mathsf{echo}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
for a quorum of participants, then
\mathsf{ready}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
.

(6)

{\mathsf{(BrEcho!)}}
says: if
\mathsf{bcst}
is
{\mathbf{t}}
for some participant and some value, then
\mathsf{echo}
is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
for some value, though not necessarily the same one.

This reflects that in the algorithm, if somebody honestly broadcasts some value and if
p
is honest, then
p
will echo some value.
The fact that this will be the
same
value (if both parties are honest) is a Lemma that follows using
{\mathsf{(BrEcho?)}}
; see Lemma
4.12
(
1
).

(7)

{\mathsf{(BrReady!!)}}
says: if
\mathsf{echo}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
for a
contraquorum
of participants, then
\mathsf{ready}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
.
This reflects the disjunct ‘or a contraquorum of ready messages’ in Remark
4.1
(
3
).

(8)

{\mathsf{(BrEcho01)}}
says that
\mathsf{echo}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
for at most one value
v
.
It asserts nothing about byzantine behaviour:
\mathsf{echo}(v)
can be
{\mathbf{b}}
for as many values as we like, reflecting the fact that dishonest participants may deviate from the protocol.

(9)

{\mathsf{(BrBroadcast1)}}
says that
\mathsf{bcst}(v)
is
{\mathbf{t}}
for precisely one value
v

or

\mathsf{bcst}(v)
is
{\mathbf{b}}
for
at least
one value
v
.
This is what

\exists_{1}

means in a three-value setting, as discussed in Remark
3.1
and Proposition
9
.

(10)

{\mathsf{(BrCorrect)}}
asserts that there are quorums of honest participants for
\mathsf{ready}
and
\mathsf{echo}
.
The literature tends to assume something slightly stronger, that there is one quorum of honest participants for all predicates.
We will only need this weaker assumption for our proofs.

(11)

{\mathsf{(BrCorrect^{\prime})}}
asserts that a participant
p
is either entirely honest for
\mathsf{ready}
and
\mathsf{echo}
, in that it returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
for every value, or it is entirely dishonest, in that it returns
{\mathbf{b}}
for every value.
This accurately reflects an informal assumption that (for a given predicate) a participant is either honest or dishonest – dishonestly is not attached to a particular message, but to the
participant
who sent it.

{\mathsf{(BrCorrect^{\prime\prime})}}
asserts that either for all participants the
\mathsf{bcst}
predicate returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
on every
v
, or for all participants it returns
{\mathbf{b}}
.
This reflects that we do not designate a unique sender:

Remark 4.5
.

Implementations of Bracha Broadcast assume a unique
sender
participant whose identity is known to all participants and whose identity cannot be faked.
The sender’s task is to pick a unique value
v
and broadcast
v
(and, if the sender is honest, only
v
) to all participants.

In our logic, we model this with a straightforward axiom
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(a)
.
Our logic is three-valued, and as per Proposition
9
(
5
) this means that there is at most one value such that somebody
{\mathbf{t}}
-sends it, but there may be many values such that somebody
{\mathbf{b}}
-sends them.
We return to this in Remark
4.11
, after we have a few more proofs.

4.2
Correctness properties for Bracha Broadcast

4.2.1
Statement of the correctness properties

Remark 4.6
.

For the rest of this section, we fix a model (Definition
11
)

\mu=(\mathsf{Value},(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open}),\varsigma)

such that the underlying semitopology
(\mathsf{P},\mathsf{Open})
is 3-twined (Definition
5
), so that we have Theorem
2.13
.

Remark 4.7
.

As promised (see end of Remark
4.1
), we will now use
ThyBB
in Figure
6
to derive predicates corresponding to the following standard correctness properties for Bracha Broadcast, per Figure
7
.
We consider them in turn:

(1)

Validity:

If a correct
p
broadcasts a value
v
, then every correct
p^{\prime}
delivers
v
.

This becomes
\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
.
See Proposition
13
.

(2)

No duplication:

Every correct
p
delivers at most one value.

This becomes
\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{dlvr}
.
See Proposition
14
.

(3)

Integrity:

If some
p
delivers a message
m
with correct sender
p^{\prime}
, then
m
was broadcast by
p^{\prime}
.

This becomes
\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{dlvr}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)
.
See Proposition
15
.

(4)

Consistency:

If correct
p
delivers
v
and another correct
p^{\prime}
delivers
v^{\prime}
then
v{=}v^{\prime}
.

This becomes
\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
.
See Proposition
14
.

(5)

Totality:

If correct
p
delivers
v
, then every correct
p^{\prime}
delivers
v
.

This becomes
\text{ThyBB}\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
.
See Proposition
16
.

The English statements above are (in order) adapted from properties BCB1, BCB2, BCB3, and BCB4 from Module 3.11, and BRB5 from Module 3.12 of
[
CGR11
]
.

Remark 4.8
.

We can make a few observations about the correctness properties above:

(1)

The logical statements are concise, precise, and (arguably) clear.

(2)

The rendering of Consistency
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
subsumes that for No Duplication
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
.
This is less visible in the English because Consistency talks about a ‘correct
p
’ and ‘another correct
p^{\prime}
’, which might be taken to indicate that
p\neq p^{\prime}
.
In our logic,
it is easier to unify Consistency and No Duplication into a single predicate and proof.

(3)

The English statement of Integrity was hard to parse, at least for me.
Contrast with the predicate
\mathsf{dlvr}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)
, which is clear and precise.

4.2.2
Validity: “if a correct
p
broadcasts
v
, then every correct
p^{\prime}
delivers
v
”

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\text{ThyBB}
.
Then precisely one of the following holds:

\forall p\in\mathsf{P}.p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{bcst}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\exists_{1}v\in\mathsf{Value}.\forall p\in\mathsf{P}.(p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v))\quad\text{or}\quad\forall v\in\mathsf{Value},p\in\mathsf{P}.(p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)).

Proof 4.10
.

{\mathsf{(BrCorrect^{\prime\prime})}}
asserts that either for all participants the
\mathsf{bcst}
predicate returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
on every
v
, or for all participants it returns
{\mathbf{b}}
.
In the latter case we have the right-hand disjunct above, and in the former case
using
{\mathsf{(BrBroadcast1)}}
we obtain the left-hand disjunct.

Remark 4.11
.

Lemma
4.9
requires some discussion.
Our description of Bracha Broadcast in Remark
4.1
assumes a designated sender with an unforgeable signature, who might be byzantine.
This is an accurate description of the implementation, but logically we do not care that there is one sender.
There could be
two
senders, so long as (if honest) they broadcast identical values.

Implementationally we guarantee this by designating a unique sender participant.
In the axiomatisation, it is convenient (and more general) to be more abstract: we axiomatise a broadcast predicate that is either somewhere
{\mathbf{t}}
for
precisely
one value (corresponding to a value broadcast by an honest sender), or it is
{\mathbf{b}}
for all values and all participants (corresponding to arbitrary hostile behaviour of a dishonest sender).
14
14

14

A longer stronger axiomatisation that is closer to the implementation could be written but would be more complex and not buy us much, since we can prove our correctness results from the weaker and simpler formulation.

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\text{ThyBB}
and
v\in\mathsf{Value}
.
Then:

(1)

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\mathsf{echo}(v)
.

(2)

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{echo}(v)
.

(3)

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{echo}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{ready}(v)
.

(4)

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{ready}(v)\mathbin{\rightarrow}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{dlvr}(v)
.

Proof 4.13
.

We consider each part in turn, freely using weak modus ponens (Prop.
2
(
4
)) and the semantics in Figure
4
:

(1)

Consider
p\in\mathsf{P}
and suppose
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)
.
By weak modus ponens (Prop.
2
(
4
)) and
{\mathsf{(BrEcho!)}}

p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{echo}(a)
.
Thus, there exists
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Value}
such that
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{echo}(v^{\prime})
.
There are now two subcases:

•

Suppose
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{echo}(v^{\prime})
.
By
{\mathsf{(BrCorrect^{\prime})}}

p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{echo}(v)
, and so
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{echo}(v)
.

•

Suppose
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{echo}(v^{\prime})
.
By Proposition
2
(
8
)
(since
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{echo}(v^{\prime})
)
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{echo}(v^{\prime})
, and so by
{\mathsf{(BrEcho?)}}

p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
4.9

v=v^{\prime}
.

(2)

From part
1
of this result, noting that the
p\in\mathsf{P}
we chose in the proof was arbitrary.

(3)

Suppose
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{echo}(v)
.
By
{\mathsf{(BrReady!)}}

p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{ready}(v)
.
This reasoning did not depend on
p
, so
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{ready}(v)
as required.

(4)

As the reasoning for part
3
, but using
{\mathsf{(BrDeliver!)}}
.

Remark 4.14
.

For clarity we spell out what Lemma
4.12
asserts.
Each part has the form
\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\phi\mathbin{\rightarrow}\psi
, meaning that for every
p\in\mathsf{P}
if
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\phi
(i.e.
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)={\mathbf{t}}
) then
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\psi
(i.e.
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mu}(p)\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
).
So part
1
means
‘
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)
implies
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{echo}(v)
’.

Note it does
not
mean ‘
p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Diamond\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{bcst}(v)
implies
{p\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{echo}(v)}
’.
That would be a different assertion.

.

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\text{ThyBB}

.

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\Box\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{ready}(v)

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{ready}(v)
.

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\mathsf{echo}(v)
and by Proposition

(

)

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\mathsf{T}\hskip 1.5pt\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{echo}(v)
.

(2)

As for part
1
, since by
{\mathsf{(BrCorrect)}}

\vDash_{\hskip-2.0pt\mu}\raisebox{0.7pt}{${\mathchoice{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\displaystyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\textstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}{\vphantom{\square}\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\mathop{\square}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\cdot$\cr}}}}$}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 1.5pt\hskip-2.0pt[\mathsf{ready}]
